=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a 2D array queries, where queries[i] is of the form [l, r]. Each queries[i] defines an array of integers nums consisting of elements ranging from l to r, both inclusive.
In one operation, you can:

Select two integers a and b from the array.
Replace them with floor(a / 4) and floor(b / 4).

Your task is to determine the minimum number of operations required to reduce all elements of the array to zero for each query. Return the sum of the results for all queries.
 
Example 1:

Input: queries = [[1,2],[2,4]]
Output: 3
Explanation:
For queries[0]:

The initial array is nums = [1, 2].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[1]. The array becomes [0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 1.

For queries[1]:

The initial array is nums = [2, 3, 4].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 3, 1].
In the second operation, select nums[1] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 2.

The output is 1 + 2 = 3.

Example 2:

Input: queries = [[2,6]]
Output: 4
Explanation:
For queries[0]:

The initial array is nums = [2, 3, 4, 5, 6].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[3]. The array becomes [0, 3, 4, 1, 6].
In the second operation, select nums[2] and nums[4]. The array becomes [0, 3, 1, 1, 1].
In the third operation, select nums[1] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0, 1, 1].
In the fourth operation, select nums[3] and nums[4]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0, 0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 4.

The output is 4.

 
Constraints:

1 <= queries.length <= 10^5
queries[i].length == 2
queries[i] == [l, r]
1 <= l < r <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Option 1: For each query, loop k=1..MAXK while 4^(k-1) <= r maybe. Since max k 15. Compute count.

Pseudo:
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        total = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            S = 0
            p = 1  # 4^(k-1)
            k = 1
            while p <= r:
                q = p * 4 - 1  # 4^k -1
                left = max(l, p)
                right = min(r, q)
                if left <= right:
                    S += k * (right - left + 1)
                p *= 4
                k += 1
            maxd = 1
            p = 4
            while r >= p:
                maxd += 1
                p *= 4
            ops = max((S + 1)//2, maxd)
            total += ops
        return total

Need ensure while p <= r for S: p=4^(k-1). For k=1 p=1, interval [1,3]. If r>=1. Good. Loop per query:
S=0
p=1
k=1
while p <= r:
    q=p*4-1
    lo=max(l,p)
    hi=min(r,q)
    if lo<=hi: S += k*(hi-lo+1)
    p*=4; k+=1
maxd=k-1
ans=max((S+1)//2, maxd)

def pref(n):
    if n <=0: return 0
    res=0
    p=1
    k=1
    while p <= n:
        res += n - p + 1  # count numbers >=p up to n
        p *=4
        k+=1
    return res

Now, let's decide final code structure:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        total = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            need_sum = 0
            p = 1  # 4^(k-1)
            k = 1
            while p <= r:
                need_sum += r - max(l, p) + 1
                p *= 4
                k += 1
            max_need = k - 1
            total += max((need_sum + 1) // 2, max_need)
        return total
```

Let's maybe write code with variable names:
```
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        ans = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            total_ops_needed = 0
            p = 1
            level = 1
            while p <= r:
                total_ops_needed += r - max(l, p) + 1
                p *= 4
                level += 1
            max_level = level - 1
            ans += max((total_ops_needed + 1) // 2, max_level)
        return ans
```
This is clear.

Let's test with [1,2] using code:
p=1, level=1, total +=2-1+1=2, p=4, level=2, loop false, max_level=1, ans += max(1,1)=1.
Wait total_ops_needed for [1,2] is 2, but d values 1+1=2. Good.

Now, let's think about if `total_ops_needed` should be named `need_sum` to avoid confusion with operations. Fine.

Potential issue: The threshold count `r - max(l, p) + 1` counts numbers in [l,r] that are >= p. But if p=1, all numbers count. If l=1,r=2 count2. Good.
For p=4, count numbers >=4. If r=4,l=2 count1. Good.

Let's maybe try to prove the scheduling theorem in a simple way for our own confidence:
Given counts c_i, T = max(ceil(S/2), max c_i). We need assign each of S unit tasks to one of T time slots and one of two positions, with no two tasks of same job in same slot. Equivalent to construct a multigraph with vertices jobs, edges representing pairs processed in same operation, plus maybe loops? Actually each operation with two distinct jobs is an edge between them; an operation with one job and zero is a "half-edge" or pendant. We need a multigraph with T edges (including half-edges) where degree of vertex i = c_i, and no loops. A half-edge contributes 1 to degree and uses one operation with only one job. If T = ceil(S/2), there are at most one half-edge if S odd, else none. If T > ceil(S/2), there are h = 2T - S half-edges? Wait each operation with two jobs contributes degree2; with one job contributes degree1. Total degree S. Number of operations T. Let e = number of full edges, h = number of half-edges. e + h = T, 2e + h = S. Thus h = 2T - S. If T = ceil(S/2), h is 0 or1. If T = max_need > ceil(S/2), h can be large. Need multigraph with degrees c_i, e full edges, h half-edges, no loops. Is existence guaranteed by conditions? This is a graph realization problem. Conditions for multigraph with given degrees and number of edges? Since multiedges allowed, no loops, half-edges allowed. We can always realize if max degree <= e + h? Let's see. A half-edge is like an edge to a dummy vertex 0 with degree h? Actually each half-edge connects a job to a dummy "zero" vertex. If we add a dummy vertex with degree h (number of half-edges), then we need a multigraph with vertices jobs + dummy, degrees c_i and h, with no loops, and edges are operations (each edge connects two vertices; edges incident to dummy are filler operations). Total edges T, sum degrees = S+h = 2T. Conditions for multigraph without loops: max degree <= sum of other degrees. Is that satisfied? For any job i, c_i <= T = sum other degrees? Since c_i <= max_need <= T. Sum other degrees = S - c_i + h? Wait including dummy degree h, total degree 2T. For vertex i, degree c_i. Need c_i <= (2T - c_i) (sum of others) => 2c_i <= 2T => c_i <= T. True. For dummy degree h, need h <= 2T - h => h <= T, true since h=2T-S <= T (because S>=T? Is S>=T? Since T=ceil(S/2) or max, T<=S? For S>=1, ceil(S/2)<=S, max<=S? max_need<=S because at least one element with max count, S>=max. So T<=S, h=2T-S <= T). Thus a multigraph with no loops exists (by Erdős–Gallai for multigraph? With multiedges allowed, the only condition is max degree <= sum others; no loops). Then each edge is an operation pairing the two vertices; if one vertex is dummy zero, operation uses one job and zero. This is a neat proof! It also handles zero filler by dummy vertex. Great. This confirms formula.

Let's verify graph conditions with dummy:
- Jobs vertices with degrees c_i.
- Dummy vertex Z with degree h = 2T - S (number of filler selections).
- Total degree = S + h = 2T, so number of edges = T.
- Need no loops (edge between same vertex and itself) and no multiple? Multiple edges allowed (same pair can be selected in multiple operations). No loops means no operation selects same element twice. For dummy, no loop means no operation selects two zeros? Actually an edge Z-Z would be an operation selecting two zeros, which is useless and not needed. We can avoid loops if degree conditions allow. With multiedges allowed, a loop would contribute 2 to degree of one vertex and use one edge, but we can avoid by construction if max degree <= sum others. Is that condition sufficient for multigraph without loops? For multigraph with no loops and arbitrary multiplicities, yes: you can connect the highest-degree vertex to others until its degree exhausted; because max degree <= sum others, you can always pair it with others, then continue. This is like handshaking lemma. For dummy degree h, h <= T ensures dummy can be paired with jobs, not itself. Since h = 2T - S. If h > T, would require dummy loop, but h<=T. Good.
This graph proof is elegant and shows sufficiency including zero filler. Nice.

Let's test graph construction for d=[5,5,5], T=8, S=15, h=1. Degrees jobs 5,5,5, dummy1. Sum16, edges8. Max job degree5 <= sum others 5+5+1=11; dummy1<=15. Multigraph exists. Schedule earlier.

So formula is definitely correct.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with using dummy zero degree h = 2T - S. If h is large, dummy zero vertex represents selecting zero as filler. In actual array, one zero element can be selected multiple times across operations; the dummy vertex with degree h corresponds to h filler selections, possibly all using the same zero element. Is that allowed? Yes, a zero element can be selected repeatedly; it remains zero. The graph edge between job i and dummy represents an operation selecting job i and some zero element. Multiple edges to dummy mean selecting a zero element multiple times. If there is at least one zero element. But if h>0, does a zero element exist at the time of each filler operation? The graph construction doesn't specify temporal order. However, we can schedule edges in an order that creates a zero before first dummy edge? Is that always possible? We need ensure. In graph terms, a dummy edge is an operation with one job and a zero. If no zero has been created yet, cannot. But we can order edges so that some job completes before dummy edges. Is it always possible? Let's examine graph with dummy degree h. If h>0, there is at least one dummy edge. We need at least one job to complete before the first dummy edge. A job completes after all edges incident to it have been processed. If a job has no dummy edges and degree c_i, we can process all its edges early to complete it, then use it as zero. Is there always a job with no dummy edges? Dummy edges are incident to some jobs; if h>0, maybe all jobs have dummy edges? Example d=[10,1,1], T=10, h=8. Graph: A-dummy8, A-B1, A-C1. Job B has no dummy, degree1; process A-B first, B completes and becomes zero. Then dummy edges okay. Good.
Could there be a case where every job has at least one dummy edge? Since h = 2T - S. If h > 0, maybe all jobs incident to dummy. Example d=[2,2,2]? S=6,T=3,h=0 no. d=[3,3,3]? h=1, graph can have dummy incident to one job only, others no dummy. We can choose. In graph realization, we can choose which vertices incident to dummy. We can choose a job with small degree to be the zero creator? Actually dummy edges represent filler selections; we can choose to pair dummy with the max job after others complete, or with a small job? If a small job is incident to dummy, it would be selected as filler while still nonzero? Wait dummy edge means selecting a zero, not the job. In graph, edge between job i and dummy means operation selects job i and a zero. The job i is processed; the zero is filler. The dummy vertex is the filler zero, not the job. If a job has dummy edges, it is processed in those operations along with zero. It doesn't create zero; it may complete after its last incident edge. To create a zero before dummy edges, we need some job to complete before dummy edges are processed. If every job has dummy edges, can we still create zero? We can order a job's non-dummy edges first, but if it has dummy edges, it won't complete until after its dummy edges. However, maybe another job completes. Need ensure at least one job with no dummy edges or whose dummy edges can be delayed until after completion? If a job has dummy edges, it cannot complete before processing them, so it can't be the first zero. But another job without dummy edges could complete. Is there always at least one job with no dummy edges if h>0? Not necessarily? Let's try construct counts where h>0 and all jobs have dummy edges in any realization? We can choose realization to avoid dummy on some job. Since h = 2T - S. If h > 0, there are h filler selections. We can always assign all dummy edges to the max job? In graph construction, dummy degree h must be realized by edges to jobs. We can choose to connect dummy only to one job A if h <= degree of A? Actually an edge between A and dummy contributes 1 to A's degree. If we connect dummy to A h times, A's degree increases by h, but A's total degree must equal c_A. So we can only connect dummy to A at most c_A times. If h <= c_A, we can assign all dummy edges to A. Is h <= c_A for some job? h = 2T - S. If T = max_need = M, h = 2M - S. Since S = M + B, h = M - B. If B < M, h >0 and h <= M = max count. So assign all dummy edges to a max-count job A. Then other jobs have no dummy edges. If T = ceil(S/2), h is 0 or1. If h=1, S odd. Need assign dummy edge to some job with count >=1 (all jobs). Other jobs may have no dummy. So there is at least one job with no dummy if there are at least two jobs? If h=1 and only one job? Not allowed. If multiple jobs, choose a job with count? If we assign dummy to one job, other jobs no dummy. So yes, we can choose a job without dummy edges. Then process that job's edges first to create zero. But wait if the job without dummy edges has count c_j, processing its c_j edges requires pairing it with other jobs. During those operations, other jobs also get processed. After c_j operations, that job completes and becomes zero. Then dummy edges can be processed. This works if c_j operations are available before dummy edges. Since total T operations, c_j <= T. Good. So zero availability can be handled by choosing a zero-creator job with no dummy edges and scheduling its incident edges first. But if the zero-creator job's incident edges include edges to other jobs that also have dummy edges, those other jobs won't complete yet, but zero exists. Good.

Thus graph proof plus ordering works. But final code no proof.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "minimum number of operations" if the array length is 2 and one element d=15, other d=1, ans
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        total = 0

        for l, r in queries:
            need_sum = 0
            p = 1          # 4^(k-1)
            max_need = 0

            while p <= r:
                # Numbers in [l, r] that need at least k operations
                need_sum += r - max(l, p) + 1
                max_need += 1
                p *= 4

            ops = max((need_sum + 1) // 2, max_need)
            total += ops

        return total
```
